Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n    20→- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository. \n    21→- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n    22→- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n    23→\n    24→## Reference Information\n    25→\n    26→- The reference **paper** \"longitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p\" is in the file `paper.pdf`.\n    27→- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`.\n    28→- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `code`.\n    29→- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `data`.\n    30→\n    31→## Decoder Task\n    32→Decode information regarding animal motion from the neural activities recorded from mouse barrel cortex.\n    33→\n    34→### Decoder Inputs:\n    35→Time elapsed from the beginning of the experiment. Time-varying.\n    36→\n    37→### Decoder Outputs\n    38→Motion energy, normalized and discretized into five equal-percentile bins. Time-varying.\n    39→\n    40→## Target Data Format\n    41→\n    42→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n    43→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    44→\n    45→```python\n    46→data = {\n    47→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    48→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    49→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    50→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    51→            ...\n    52→        ],\n    53→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    54→            ...\n    55→        ],\n    56→        ...\n    57→    ],\n    58→\n    59→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    60→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    61→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    62→            ...\n    63→        ],\n    64→        ...\n    65→    ],\n    66→\n    67→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    68→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    69→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    70→            ...\n    71→        ],\n    72→        ...\n    73→    ],\n    74→\n    75→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    76→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    77→\n    78→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    79→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    80→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    81→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    82→\n    83→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    84→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    85→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n    86→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n    87→        ...\n    88→    ],\n    89→\n    90→    'metadata': {\n    91→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n    92→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n    93→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n    94→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    95→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    96→        ...\n    97→    }\n    98→}\n    99→```\n   100→\n   101→### Key Format Requirements\n   102→\n   103→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   104→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   105→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   106→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   107→\n   108→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   109→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   110→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   111→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   112→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   113→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   114→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   115→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   116→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   117→\n   118→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   119→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   120→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   121→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   122→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   123→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   124→\n   125→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   126→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   127→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   128→\n   129→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   130→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   131→\n   132→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   133→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   134→\n   135→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   136→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   137→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   138→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   139→\n   140→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   141→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   142→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   143→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   144→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   145→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   146→\n   147→## Python environment\n   148→\n   149→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   150→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   151→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   152→\n   153→## Decoder Reference\n   154→\n   155→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   156→- Optional arguments:\n   157→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   158→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   159→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   160→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   161→\n   162→### Purpose\n   163→\n   164→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   165→1. Experimental and behavioral variables\n   291→  - Data processing\n   292→  - Data curation\n   293→- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n   294→\n   295→**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n   296→\n   297→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   298→\n   299→---\n   300→\n   301→### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   302→\n   303→**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n   304→\n   305→**Actions**:\n   306→- Map source data elements to target format components:\n   307→  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n   308→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   309→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   310→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   311→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   312→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   313→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \n   314→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   315→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   316→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   317→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   318→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   319→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   320→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   321→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   322→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   323→\n   324→**Done when**:\n   325→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   326→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   327→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   328→\n   329→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   330→\n   331→---\n   332→\n   333→### Step 6: Script Development\n   334→\n   335→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\n   336→\n   337→**Actions**:\n   338→- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\n   339→- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   340→- Include options:\n   341→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   342→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   343→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   344→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   345→- Write efficient code:\n   346→  - Vectorize loops\n   347→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   348→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   349→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   350→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   351→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   352→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   353→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   354→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   355→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   356→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   357→\n   358→**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   359→\n   360→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   361→\n   362→---\n   363→\n   364→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   365→\n   366→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   367→\n   368→**Actions**:\n   369→1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   370→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   371→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   372→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   373→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   374→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   375→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   376→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   377→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   378→3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   379→4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   380→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   381→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   382→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   383→\n   384→**Done when**: \n   385→- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   386→- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   387→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   388→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   389→\n   390→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   391→\n   392→---\n   393→\n   394→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   395→\n   396→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   397→\n   398→**Actions**:\n   399→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   400→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   401→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   402→\n   403→**Investigate any issues**:\n   404→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   405→\n   406→**Done when**: \n   407→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   408→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   409→- Accuracies are above chance\n   410→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   411→\n   412→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   413→\n   414→---\n   415→\n   416→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   417→\n   418→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   419→\n   420→**Actions**:\n   421→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   422→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   423→3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n   424→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   425→5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n   426→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   427→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   428→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   429→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   430→\n   431→**Done when**:\n   432→- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   433→- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   434→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   435→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   436→\n   437→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   438→\n   439→---\n   440→\n   441→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   442→\n   443→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   444→\n   445→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   446→Read through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   447→\n   448→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   449→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   450→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   451→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   452→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   453→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   454→- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   455→\n   456→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   457→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   458→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   459→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   460→- Fix any mismatches. \n   461→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   462→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   463→\n   464→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   465→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \n   466→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   467→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   468→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   469→\n   470→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   471→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   472→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   473→\n   474→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   475→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   476→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   477→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   478→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   479→\n   480→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   481→\n   482→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   483→\n   484→---\n   485→\n   486→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   487→\n   488→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   489→\n   490→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   491→\n   492→**Actions**:\n   493→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   494→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   495→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   496→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \n   497→\n   498→**Done when**: \n   499→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   500→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   501→\n   502→---\n   503→\n   504→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   505→\n   506→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   507→\n   508→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   509→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   510→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   511→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   512→\n   513→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\n   514→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\n   515→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\n   516→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   517→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   518→\n   519→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   520→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   521→\n   522→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   523→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   524→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   525→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   526→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   527→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \n   528→\n   529→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   530→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   531→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   532→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   533→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   534→\n   535→**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   536→\n   537→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   538→\n   539→---\n   540→\n   541→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   542→\n   543→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   544→\n   545→**Actions**:\n   546→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   547→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   548→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   549→  - Validation results and tables\n   550→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   551→- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n   552→  - Dataset description\n   553→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   554→  - Output format specification\n   555→  - Key statistics\n   556→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n   557→- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   558→\n   559→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   560→\n   561→---\n   562→\n   563→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   564→\n   565→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. data\n   817→- **Trial alignment**: Align trials to meaningful events (stimulus onset, movement, etc.)\n   818→- **Time windows**: Choose appropriate pre/post-event windows\n   819→- **Neuron curation**: Choose filtering criteria for the quality of each neuron's recording based on reference texts\n   820→- **Trial curation**: Choose quality filtering criteria for trials based on reference texts or bad or missing data\n   821→\n   822→### Computational Efficiency\n   823→- Be mindful of memory usage with large datasets\n   824→- Use appropriate data types (float32 vs. float64)\n   825→- Consider lazy loading for very large datasets\n   826→- Implement efficient array operations (vectorization)\n   827→\n   828→## Success Criteria\n   829→\n   830→A successful conversion should:\n   831→1. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference paper and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"longitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p\" is in the file `paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`.\n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\nDecode information regarding animal motion from the neural activities recorded from mouse barrel cortex.\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\nTime elapsed from the beginning of the experiment. Time-varying.\n\n### Decoder Outputs\nMotion energy, normalized and discretized into five equal-percentile bins. Time-varying.\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5.  91→    if not np.isnan(x).any():\n    92→        return x\n    93→    idx = np.arange(x.shape[0])\n    94→    valid = ~np.isnan(x)\n    95→    if not valid.any():\n    96→        raise return out\n   100→\n   101→\n   102→def reconstruct_motion_trace(motion: np.ndarray, interframe_int: np.ndarray, target_len: int) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\n   103→    motion = np.asarray(motion, dtype=np.float32)\n   104→    interframe_int = np.asarray(interframe_int, dtype=np.float64)\n   105→    if motion.shape[0] == target_len:\n   106→        return motion, np.empty(0, dtype=np.int64)\n   107→\n   108→    median_interval = float(np.median(interframe_int))\n   109→    steps = np.rint(interframe_int / median_interval).astype(np.int64)\n   110→    steps[steps < 1] =  )\n   120→\n   121→    full = np.full(target_len, np.nan, dtype=np.float32)\n   122→    full[observed_idx] = motion\n   123→    missing_idx = np.flatnonzero(np.isnan(full))\n   124→    full = 163→\n   164→\n   165→def segment_trials(\n   166→    neural_binned: np.ndarray,\n   167→    time_binned: np.ndarray,\n   168→    motion_norm_binned: np.ndarray,\n   169→    motion_edges: NaNs.\")\n    out = x.copy()\n    out[~valid] = np.interp(idx[~valid], idx[valid], x[valid]).astype(np.float32)\n    return out\n\n\ndef reconstruct_motion_trace(motion: np.ndarray, interframe_int: np.ndarray, target_len: int) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\n    motion = np.asarray(motion, dtype=np.float32)\n    interframe_int = np.asarray(interframe_int, dtype=np.float64)\n    if motion.shape[0] == target_len:\n        return motion, np.empty(0, dtype=np.int64)\n\n    median_interval = float(np.median(interframe_int))\n    steps = np.rint(interframe_int / )\n\n    full = np.full(target_len, np.nan, dtype=np.float32)\n    full[observed_idx] = motion\n    missing_idx = np.flatnonzero(np.isnan(full))\n    full = interpolate_nans(full)\n    return full, This exactly matches the paper’s decoder split granularity and gives 10 trials for 20-minute sessions and 15 trials for 30-minute sessions.\n   194→3. **Temporal binning**: Average non-overlapping 10-frame windows for neural and behavior streams before segmentation, matching the paper’s decoder denoising (`10 consecutive timestamps`). This yields `333.33 ms` bins.\n   195→4. **Master clock**: Use imaging frames as the reference timeline because `ops.npy` gives authoritative `fs=30` and sessions have fixed imaging lengths. Reinsert missing behavior frames from timing gaps so both modalities remain frame-aligned before binning.\n   196→5. **Behavior output representation**: Because the target decoder requires categorical outputs, convert the denoised motion-energy trace into 5 global equal-percentile classes. Use global min-max normalization only to satisfy the task wording; percentile thresholds are based on the normalized values, which preserves ordering.\n   197→6. **Input representation**: Encode elapsed session time as one continuous, time-varying input in seconds. Each pseudo-trial keeps its absolute position within the original recording, so the decoder receives the requested “time elapsed from the beginning of the experiment.”\n   198→7. **Session inclusion**: Keep all 41 sessions. No session fails the core reference criteria, and all sessions yield at least 10 pseudo-trials after processing.\n   199→8. **Neuron inclusion**: Keep all neurons present in the released matched Suite2p arrays for each subject. These already represent the Track2p all-days matched population analyzed in the paper.\n   200→\n   201→### Planned Sanity Checks\n   202→- [ ] Neural preprocessing spot-check: for a chosen bundled code is the Track2p package, not a full experiment-analysis repo. It explains how longitudinally matched neurons are created from per-session Suite2p outputs.\n- Core curation rule in code: ROIs are filtered by `iscell.npy`. If `track_ops.iscell_thr is None`, use binary `iscell[:,0] == 1`; otherwise use probability `iscell[:,1] > track_ops.iscell_thr`. The default threshold in `DefaultTrackOps` is `0.50`.\n- Longitudinal cell set for downstream analysis is `t2p_match_mat_allday = t2p_match_mat[~np.any(t2p_match_mat == None, axis=1), :]`, i.e. only rows with a matched ROI on every day are retained.\n- Available neural traces in the GUI export/import path are raw fluorescence `F`, deconvolved `spks`, and a `dF/F0` option.\n- The `dF/F0` option in `DataManagement.F_processing` does not divide by baseline; it performs `Fc = F - neucoeff * Fneu` with default `neucoeff=0.0`, then subtracts a maximin baseline (`gaussian_filter`, `minimum_filter1d`, `maximum_filter1d`). This is important because the code’s named `dF/F0` is effectively baseline-subtracted fluorescence, not a classical ratio.\n- No trial structure, behavior variables, motion-energy extraction, or decoder code appears in `code/`. paper/methods)\n| Statistic | Value | Source Quote |\n|-----------|-------|--------------|\n| Neurons (total) | Approximately 526 tracked neurons per mouse on average across all days | `On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse` |\n| Neurons / session | Several hundreds; matched cells persist across all days of a mouse | `several hundreds of individual neurons` |\n| Subjects | 6 mice | `a full dataset of 6 mice` |\n| Sessions / subject | Minimum 6 consecutive daily sessions; example datasets span 7 days | `daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days` |\n| Trials (total) | Not trial-based in source; continuous 20 minute recordings | `each session lasted 20 minutes` |\n| Trials / session | No native trials reported | `each session lasted 20 minutes` |\n| Neural data time bin | Raw acquisition 30 Hz (33.3 ms / frame) | `Imaging rate was 30 Hz` |\n| Behavior data time bin | Raw videography 30 Hz, microscope-triggered | `Videos were recorded at 30 Hz` |\n| Reward rate | N/A; spontaneous behavior task, no reward | `spontaneously run on a non-motorised treadmill` | \n| Motion metric | Global motion energy from squared frame differences | `pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames` | \n| Decoder smoothing bin | 10 consecutive timestamps (~333 ms) | `averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps` |\n| Decoder evaluation blocks | Consecutive 2 minute blocks | `splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks` |\n\n\n### Processing Details\n- Recordings are spontaneous-behavior calcium imaging from mouse barrel cortex, layer 2/3, during the second postnatal week.\n- Imaging and videography are both sampled at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition triggering the camera, so the intended reference alignment is framewise synchrony between neural and behavior streams.\n- Calcium preprocessing in the reference study:\n  - Suite2p per recording for motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction, and spike deconvolution.\n  - Cell inclusion threshold: use ROIs with Suite2p cell probability above 0.5.\n  - Downstream analyses in the paper use baseline-corrected fluorescence traces as dF/F, described as using default Suite2p parameters.\n- Behavioral preprocessing in the reference study:\n  - Motion energy is computed from consecutive video frames, as squared pixelwise differences summed over pixels, producing one scalar per frame.\n- Decoding in the reference study:\n  - Predict behavioral motion from neural population activity using ridge regression.\n  - Same-day decoding uses nested 5-fold cross-validation.\n  - Splits are consecutive 2 minute blocks.\n  - Neural and behavior traces are both slightly denoised by averaging over 10-frame bins before decoding.\n  - Performance is reported as `R^2`, with same-day decoding improving with development and cross-day decoding becoming accurate at later ages.\n\n### Curation Steps\n\n**Neuron curation rules**:\n- Keep ROIs classified as cells by Suite2p with probability threshold `> 0.5`.\n- Track2p is then used to identify the subset of neurons present across all days of a mouse for longitudinal analyses.\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\n- No trial-based curation is described because the recordings are continuous spontaneous-behavior sessions rather than discrete trials.\n- Behavioral frames can be missing in some sessions; the data README says missing frames should be identified from `tstamps.npy` / `interframe_int.npy` and treated as missing values or interpolated.\n\n### Decoders Trained\n| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n| Mouse motion / motion energy | No exact text-table value in the paper text; performance reported as `R^2`, increasing with age and showing accurate late cross-day decoding |\n\n---\n\n## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n**Status**: Consistent. Treat bundled neural data as already Track2p-matched and cell-filtered. |\n| Neural trace type | GUI offers `F`, `spks`, or a `dF/F0` helper implemented as baseline subtraction with `neucoeff=0.0` | Sessions contain `F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `spks.npy`, not precomputed `dF/F.npy` | Paper states analyses used baseline-corrected fluorescence traces as dF/F with default Suite2p parameters | Use paper-consistent Suite2p-style preprocessing from `F` and `Fneu`; do not use GUI `F_processing` as the reference analysis path. |\n| Matched-cell population | Track2p export keeps cells present across all days (`t2p_match_mat_allday`) | Within each subject all sessions have identical neuron counts and row identities | Paper states functional analyses used neurons successfully tracked across all days | Consistent. Subject-level neuron identity is row-aligned across days. |\n| Neural/behavior alignment | Track2p code does not handle behavior | Most sessions have matching imaging and behavior lengths; 9 sessions have shorter behavior arrays | Camera triggered by microscope at 30 Hz; README notes missing camera frames and says to infer them from `tstamps.npy` / `interframe_int.npy` | Treat imaging frames as the master clock and reconstruct missing behavior frames from doubled timing gaps before alignment. |\n| Dataset scale | Example defaults in code show only 3 sessions for one toy subject | Data release has 6 subjects and 41 sessions | Paper reports 6 mice with at least 6 daily sessions | Use full release dataset, not code defaults. Data counts are consistent with the paper. |\n| Summary neuron counts | Code does not state paper summary statistics | Unique tracked neurons per mouse in release: `[221, 370, 685, 746, 541, 435]`, mean `499.7`, sample std `197.7` | Paper reports mean `526 ± 190` tracked neurons per mouse | Close agreement; small difference is acceptable and likely due to dataset subset / rounding in paper. |\n\nFinal understanding:\n- Source neural data are continuous 30 Hz daily recordings from barrel cortex, already matched across days within a subject.\n- The authoritative neural preprocessing target is Suite2p-style baseline-corrected fluorescence using default Suite2p parameters, because that is what the paper states for downstream analyses.\n- Behavioral motion energy is a framewise scalar aligned to imaging frames, except for missing camera frames that must be reinserted using timing gaps.\n- There is no native trial structure, so any trialization needed for the target decoder format must be an explicit downstream segmentation of continuous recordings rather than recovery of hidden experimental trials.\n\n---\n\n## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Variable Mapping\n| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n| `suite2p/plane0/F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `ops.npy` | `neural` | Compute Suite2p-style baseline-corrected fluorescence: `Fc = F - neucoeff * Fneu`, then `suite2p.extraction.dcnv.preprocess(...)`; average non-overlapping 10-frame bins; split continuous session into consecutive 2-minute blocks | Track2p provides loading only; paper/methods specify downstream dF/F usage | Use per-session `ops.npy` values: `neucoeff=0.7`, `baseline=maximin`, `win_baseline=60`, `sig_baseline=10`, `prctile_baseline=8`, `fs=30` |\n| Session frame index / bin centers | `input[0]` | Convert to elapsed time in seconds from session start; after 10-frame averaging, use bin-center time for each sample inside each 2-minute block | No direct reference code; required by decoder task | Time-varying single input dimension |\n| `move_deve/motion_energy_glob.npy`, `tstamps.npy`, `interframe_int.npy` | `output[0]` | Reconstruct missing frames from doubled timing gaps so behavior length matches imaging; average non-overlapping 10-frame bins; global min-max normalize valid values; discretize all valid binned samples into 5 equal-percentile bins; split into same 2-minute blocks as neural data | Paper/methods define motion energy; data README defines missing-frame handling | Single categorical output dimension with classes `Q1`-`Q5` |\n| Subject folder name (e.g. `jm031`) | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Unique subject list in sorted order; session indices point into this list | N/A | 6 subjects |\n| Constant anatomical label from paper (`barrel cortex`, layer 2/3) | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | One region label for all neurons in all sessions | Paper methods | Use one region entry for the whole dataset |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Neural trace choice**: Use paper-consistent Suite2p-style baseline-corrected fluorescence rather than raw `F`, `spks`, or the GUI `dF/F0` helper, because the paper explicitly states downstream analyses used baseline-corrected fluorescence traces with default Suite2p parameters.\n2. **Trialization strategy**: Convert each continuous session into consecutive 2-minute pseudo-trials after 10-frame averaging. This exactly matches the paper’s decoder split granularity and gives 10 trials for 20-minute sessions and 15 trials for 30-minute sessions.\n3. **Temporal binning**: Average non-overlapping 10-frame windows for neural and behavior streams before segmentation, matching the paper’s decoder denoising (`10 consecutive timestamps`). This yields `333.33 ms` bins.\n4. **Master clock**: Use imaging frames as the reference timeline because `ops.npy` gives authoritative `fs=30` and sessions have fixed imaging lengths. Reinsert missing behavior frames from timing gaps so both modalities remain frame-aligned before binning.\n5. **Behavior output representation**: Because the target decoder requires categorical outputs, convert the denoised motion-energy trace into 5 global equal-percentile classes. Use global min-max normalization only to satisfy the task wording; percentile thresholds are based on the normalized values, which preserves ordering.\n6. **Input representation**: Encode elapsed session time as one continuous, time-varying input in seconds. Each pseudo-trial keeps its absolute position within the original recording, so the decoder receives the requested “time elapsed from the beginning of the experiment.”\n7. **Session inclusion**: Keep all 41 sessions. No session fails the core reference criteria, and all sessions yield at least 10 pseudo-trials after processing.\n8. **Neuron inclusion**: Keep all neurons present in the released matched Suite2p arrays for each subject. These already represent the Track2p all-days matched population analyzed in the paper.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] Neural preprocessing spot-check: for a chosen session/neuron/window, compare converted neural values against a direct manual computation from raw `F`, `Fneu`, and `ops` using `np.allclose()`.\n- [ ] Behavior reconstruction spot-check: for a mismatch session, reconstruct the full-length motion trace directly from raw `motion_energy_glob`, `tstamps`, and `interframe_int` and verify selected samples in the converted output with `np.allclose()`.\n- [ ] Binning and segmentation check: manually average one raw 10-frame block and verify it matches the first converted time bin for neural, input time, and output class assignment.\n- [ ] Dataset statistics check: verify subject count, session count, per-subject neuron counts, and mean tracked neurons per mouse are consistent with paper-reported scale.\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\nImplementation notes:\n- `convert_data.py` created.\n- Current implementation:\n  - discovers sessions from `data/`\n  - reconstructs missing behavior frames from timing gaps\n  - computes Suite2p-style baseline-corrected fluorescence using saved `ops.npy` parameters\n  - averages neural, behavior, and time streams in non-overlapping 10-frame bins\n  - segments each session into consecutive 2-minute pseudo-trials\n  - normalizes motion energy globally and discretizes it into 5 equal-percentile bins\n  - writes the target pickle format and optional processing plots\n- Smoke test passed on `--sample --show-processing`:\n  - 2 sessions processed successfully\n  - 25 pseudo-trials created\n  - 2 processing plots written\n  - mean processing time was `2.78 s / session`\n\nCode inefficiencies identified:\n- Full dataset not yet benchmarked, but the main cost is Suite2p-style fluorescence preprocessing.\n\nCode speedups added:\n- Session-wise processing only; It provides compelling evidence demonstrating reliable\n    16→longitudinal identification of neurons across the second postnatal week in mice. The\n    17→study should be of interest to development neuroscientists engaged in population-\n    18→level recordings using two-photon imaging.\n    19→https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2.sa4\n    20→Abstract\n    21→Understanding cortical circuit development requires tracking neuronal activity across days in\n    22→the growing brain. While in vivo calcium imaging now enables such longitudinal studies,\n    23→automated tools for reliably tracking large populations of neurons across sessions remain\n    24→limited. Here, we present a novel cell-tracking method based on sequential image\n    25→registration, validated on calcium imaging data from the barrel cortex of mouse pups over\n    26→one postnatal week. Our approach enables robust long-term analysis of several hundreds of\n    27→individual neurons, allowing quantification of neuronal dynamics and representational\n    28→stability over time. Using this method, we identified a key developmental transition in\n    29→neuronal activity statistics, marking the emergence of arousal state modulation. Beyond this\n    30→key finding, our method provides an essential tool for tracking developmental trajectories of\n    31→individual neurons, which could help identify potential deviations associated with\n    32→neurodevelopmental disorders.\n    33→Introduction\n    34→Early postnatal development in rodents is a period of intense circuit wiring and remodelling at\n    35→several scales through various major processes that include neuronal growth, synaptogenesis,\n    36→apoptosis, migration, rise of intracortical connectivity, functional maturation of inhibitory\n    37→Reviewed Preprint\n    38→v2 • October 27, 2025\n    39→Revised by authors\n    40→Reviewed Preprint\n    41→v1 • July 9, 2025\n    42→Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 2 of 46\n    43→synapses and the disappearance of transient connectivity schemes 1     –4     . Critical periods for\n    44→various sensory systems open and close during this time, further highlighting the profound\n    45→reshaping of cortical networks 4     . This developmental period of remodelling is crucial for\n    46→establishing the functional architecture of the mature cortex. Most importantly, all of these\n    47→developmental processes are activity-dependent, with collective dynamics playing a critical role in\n    48→the proper integration of neurons into functional networks 5     . These network dynamics\n    49→sequentially unfold while relying on different mechanisms and circuits for their generation\n    50→1     ,5     . The emergence of functional brain circuits during development is therefore a precisely\n    51→timed choreography, the timing of which is inherently tied to the age of the organism under study.\n    52→However, developmental age lacks precision due to significant variations in physical\n    53→characteristics and growth patterns, even among offspring from the same genetic lineage (Fig.\n    54→1     ). Hence, longitudinal imaging from the same animal across days is the optimal solution to\n    55→better capture the evolution of circuit dynamics during development. Additionally, developmental\n    56→variability extends beyond the organism level, becoming even more pronounced at the single\n    57→neuron level (Fig. 1     ). While population-level descriptions of cortical circuit development are\n    58→crucial, individual neurons exhibit unique developmental trajectories rooted in their specific\n    59→origin, birth timing and cellular identity 1     . In addition, the singular dynamics of sparse\n    60→individual neurons can matter, as demonstrated for the rare hub cells 6     ,7     . Thus, in order to\n    61→fully understand the circuit basis of cortical development in health and disease, it is crucial to\n    62→track neuronal activity at both population and individual cell levels in the growing brain of the\n    63→same animal. This hurdle spans both experimental methodology and data analysis.\n    64→Significant progress has been made over the recent years to develop innovative solutions to meet\n    65→this challenge on the experimental side, using two-photon calcium imaging. Technical\n    66→improvements included modified head plates and specialized surgical and care protocols 6     ,8     –\n    67→12     . Still, in many cases, different neurons from the same mouse were recorded at different ages,\n    68→or the tracking of individual cells could only be achieved through visual inspection and manual\n    69→annotation of relatively sparse, often genetically-subscribed neuronal populations expressing a\n    70→calcium fluorescent reporter. The gold standard for developmental circuit analysis would be\n    71→automated tracking of densely labeled neuronal populations, enabling efficient longitudinal\n    72→monitoring while eliminating the burden of manual cell tracking. An algorithm capable of\n    73→accurate automatic tracking of the same neurons across multiple days of brain development is\n    74→thus essential.\n    75→Methods for tracking the activity of large populations of cells across days have been successfully\n    76→developed and deployed in the adult brain 13     –16     . In adult circuits, cell tracking is indeed\n    77→relatively straightforward due to their structural stability, with minimal tissue growth, negligible\n    78→morphological changes, and constant numbers of neurons. In contrast, the first weeks of mouse\n    79→neocortical development are characterized by rapid and critical developmental changes spanning\n    80→several scales from synapses, to single neurons and networks. These include extensive brain\n    81→growth 17     ,18     , substantial morphological changes 19      as well as changes to cell numbers due\n    82→to programmed cell death 10     . This makes tracking the same cells across sessions substantially\n    83→more difficult during development compared to the adult brain.\n    84→To overcome the technical limitations detailed above, we developed an experimental protocol\n    85→using chronic calcium imaging in mice and a novel cell tracking algorithm (Track2p), specifically\n    86→tailored to development. This allowed us to track the activity of large, densely labelled,\n    87→populations of neurons during early postnatal development, which has not been possible before.\n    88→The algorithm overcomes the challenges of cell tracking during brain growth by applying\n    89→sequential registration and cell matching steps, using the spatial overlap of cells on adjacent\n    90→recordings as a matching criterion. Track2p is freely available as an open-source package with an\n    91→interactive GUI, enabling researchers to analyze longitudinal calcium imaging data from both\n    92→developing and mature circuits.\n    93→Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 3 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 3 of 46\n    94→Figure 1\n    95→Tracking developmental trajectories at the organismal and cellular level.\n    96→Postnatal development in mammals is not a strictly stereotyped process (left) but rather shows variability across individual\n    97→organisms as well as across individual cells of the same organism (right). Performing an acute experiment (dashed grey line)\n    98→only provides a single snapshot of the developmental trajectory of an individual organism (or cell). Alternatively, a\n    99→longitudinal experiment (grey arrow) allows to track the properties of the same individual (or cell) throughout development,\n   100→which is especially important in the case of variability in developmental trajectories (right).\n   101→Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 4 of 46\n   102→Applying the algorithm to a dataset recorded during the second postnatal week in mouse barrel\n   103→cortex, a critical period for the formation of topographic maps in that region, yielded hundreds of\n   104→identified neurons tracked across all days. Assessing the quality of the algorithm by benchmarking\n   105→it on a newly generated ground truth dataset showed high tracking performance 20     . Leveraging\n   106→ground truth benchmarking, we demonstrate that explicitly accounting for developmental\n   107→processes, such as brain growth, are critical for accurately tracking cells during postnatal\n   108→development. Our work thus shows that chronic calcium imaging and cell tracking using Track2p\n   109→can be used to monitor the changing physiological properties of large populations of matched\n   110→neurons during early cortical development. We demonstrate that the statistics of activity patterns\n   111→in the tracked population display two periods of stability, with a critical transition point around\n   112→postnatal day 11 (P11), marking the emergence of a stable behavioral state representation.\n   113→Results\n   114→Cell tracking using image registration and overlap-based matching\n   115→Tracking neuronal activity across multiple days presents unique challenges due to the dynamic\n   116→nature of brain development. We developed a novel tracking algorithm, called Track2p. As in\n   117→other tracking algorithms for calcium imaging data 13     ,16     ,21     , the final goal of Track2p is to\n   118→follow individual cells across sessions allowing the user to compare their functional properties in\n   119→downstream analyses. To achieve this goal, the algorithm takes as input a set of preprocessed\n   120→recordings, each consisting of a set of regions of interest (ROIs i.e. putative neurons detected based\n   121→on activity, see Methods) and their respective calcium fluorescence traces, as well as a mean image\n   122→of the field of view (FOV). Briefly, the algorithm aims to match ROIs in any given pair of\n   123→consecutive sessions based on their spatial overlap, assuming that the more the two overlap in\n   124→anatomical space, the more likely they correspond to the same neuron. Due to developmental\n   125→processes such as brain growth and other experimental factors, it is necessary to account for day-\n   126→to-day changes that occur between the two recordings before computing spatial overlaps. This is\n   127→achieved by performing affine image registration on the mean FOV images between consecutive\n   128→days.\n   129→We apply the registration and spatial matching iteratively, starting with the first pair of sessions\n   130→(s0 and s1) as follows (Fig. 2A     ):\n   131→Firstly, we estimate the spatial transformation between s0 and s1 using affine image registration\n   132→(Ntatsis 2023) (i.e. allowing shifting, rotation, scaling and shearing, see Fig. 2B     , the\n   133→transformation is denoted as T). We employ affine transformation, since it can account both for\n   134→rigid transformations (rotations and translations arising from minor mismatches in FOV\n   135→alignment across experiments) as well as scaling and shearing (mostly due to brain growth). The\n   136→changes across the two consecutive recordings are approximated as the transformation\n   137→registering the mean FOV from session s1 (green in Fig. 2B     ) to the mean FOV of s0 serving as\n   138→reference (red in Fig. 2B     ).\n   139→Secondly, the computed transformation is applied to the ROIs from session s1 (green in Fig. 2C     )\n   140→to align them to ROIs from session s0 (red in Fig. 2C     ). The amount of spatial overlap after\n   141→registration (yellow in Fig. 2C     , bottom), can indicate the accuracy of the estimated\n   142→transformation between the two days. Assuming that the transformation is accurately estimated,\n   143→ROIs corresponding to the same cells display substantial spatial overlap, with some ROIs from one\n   144→recording also potentially overlapping poorly, if not at all, with any ROI from another.\n   145→Thirdly, once the ROIs are aligned, the algorithm proceeds with the matching (Fig. 2D     ). This is\n   146→done by computing a spatial similarity metric (intersection over union - IoU) between each ROI\n   147→from session s0 and each transformed ROI from session s1. Matches are then assigned in a globally\n   148→Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 5 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 5 of 46\n   149→Figure 2\n   150→The Track2p pipeline for tracking cells across recordings.\n   151→A: Schematic overview of the Track2p algorithm and the GUI capabilities. Dashed squares represent optional steps in the\n   152→pipeline. B: The transformation (T) between two consecutive days is computed by registering the mean FOV images. Top: An\n   153→overlay of the reference (red, s0) and the moving (green, s1) images before registration. Bottom: Same two images after\n   154→registration. Scale bar: 50 μm. C: Applying the transform to ROIs from Suite2p segmentation. The ROI color code is the same\n   155→as in B, the intersection of the ROIs is shown in yellow. Top: Overlap before T.
