It provides compelling evidence demonstrating reliable\nlongitudinal identification of neurons across the second postnatal week in mice. The\nstudy should be of interest to development neuroscientists engaged in population-\nlevel recordings using two-photon imaging.\nhttps://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2.sa4\nAbstract\nUnderstanding cortical circuit development requires tracking neuronal activity across days in\nthe growing brain. While in vivo calcium imaging now enables such longitudinal studies,\nautomated tools for reliably tracking large populations of neurons across sessions remain\nlimited. Here, we present a novel cell-tracking method based on sequential image\nregistration, validated on calcium imaging data from the barrel cortex of mouse pups over\none postnatal week. Our approach enables robust long-term analysis of several hundreds of\nindividual neurons, allowing quantification of neuronal dynamics and representational\nstability over time. Using this method, we identified a key developmental transition in\nneuronal activity statistics, marking the emergence of arousal state modulation. Beyond this\nkey finding, our method provides an essential tool for tracking developmental trajectories of\nindividual neurons, which could help identify potential deviations associated with\nneurodevelopmental disorders.\nIntroduction\nEarly postnatal development in rodents is a period of intense circuit wiring and remodelling at\nseveral scales through various major processes that include neuronal growth, synaptogenesis,\napoptosis, migration, rise of intracortical connectivity, functional maturation of inhibitory\nReviewed Preprint\nv2 • October 27, 2025\nRevised by authors\nReviewed Preprint\nv1 • July 9, 2025\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 2 of 46\nsynapses and the disappearance of transient connectivity schemes 1     –4     . Critical periods for\nvarious sensory systems open and close during this time, further highlighting the profound\nreshaping of cortical networks 4     . This developmental period of remodelling is crucial for\nestablishing the functional architecture of the mature cortex. Most importantly, all of these\ndevelopmental processes are activity-dependent, with collective dynamics playing a critical role in\nthe proper integration of neurons into functional networks 5     . These network dynamics\nsequentially unfold while relying on different mechanisms and circuits for their generation\n1     ,5     . The emergence of functional brain circuits during development is therefore a precisely\ntimed choreography, the timing of which is inherently tied to the age of the organism under study.\nHowever, developmental age lacks precision due to significant variations in physical\ncharacteristics and growth patterns, even among offspring from the same genetic lineage (Fig.\n1     ). Hence, longitudinal imaging from the same animal across days is the optimal solution to\nbetter capture the evolution of circuit dynamics during development. Additionally, developmental\nvariability extends beyond the organism level, becoming even more pronounced at the single\nneuron level (Fig. 1     ). While population-level descriptions of cortical circuit development are\ncrucial, individual neurons exhibit unique developmental trajectories rooted in their specific\norigin, birth timing and cellular identity 1     . In addition, the singular dynamics of sparse\nindividual neurons can matter, as demonstrated for the rare hub cells 6     ,7     . Thus, in order to\nfully understand the circuit basis of cortical development in health and disease, it is crucial to\ntrack neuronal activity at both population and individual cell levels in the growing brain of the\nsame animal. This hurdle spans both experimental methodology and data analysis.\nSignificant progress has been made over the recent years to develop innovative solutions to meet\nthis challenge on the experimental side, using two-photon calcium imaging. Technical\nimprovements included modified head plates and specialized surgical and care protocols 6     ,8     –\n12     . Still, in many cases, different neurons from the same mouse were recorded at different ages,\nor the tracking of individual cells could only be achieved through visual inspection and manual\nannotation of relatively sparse, often genetically-subscribed neuronal populations expressing a\ncalcium fluorescent reporter. The gold standard for developmental circuit analysis would be\nautomated tracking of densely labeled neuronal populations, enabling efficient longitudinal\nmonitoring while eliminating the burden of manual cell tracking. An algorithm capable of\naccurate automatic tracking of the same neurons across multiple days of brain development is\nthus essential.\nMethods for tracking the activity of large populations of cells across days have been successfully\ndeveloped and deployed in the adult brain 13     –16     . In adult circuits, cell tracking is indeed\nrelatively straightforward due to their structural stability, with minimal tissue growth, negligible\nmorphological changes, and constant numbers of neurons. In contrast, the first weeks of mouse\nneocortical development are characterized by rapid and critical developmental changes spanning\nseveral scales from synapses, to single neurons and networks. These include extensive brain\ngrowth 17     ,18     , substantial morphological changes 19      as well as changes to cell numbers due\nto programmed cell death 10     . This makes tracking the same cells across sessions substantially\nmore difficult during development compared to the adult brain.\nTo overcome the technical limitations detailed above, we developed an experimental protocol\nusing chronic calcium imaging in mice and a novel cell tracking algorithm (Track2p), specifically\ntailored to development. This allowed us to track the activity of large, densely labelled,\npopulations of neurons during early postnatal development, which has not been possible before.\nThe algorithm overcomes the challenges of cell tracking during brain growth by applying\nsequential registration and cell matching steps, using the spatial overlap of cells on adjacent\nrecordings as a matching criterion. Track2p is freely available as an open-source package with an\ninteractive GUI, enabling researchers to analyze longitudinal calcium imaging data from both\ndeveloping and mature circuits.\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 3 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 3 of 46\nFigure 1\nTracking developmental trajectories at the organismal and cellular level.\nPostnatal development in mammals is not a strictly stereotyped process (left) but rather shows variability across individual\norganisms as well as across individual cells of the same organism (right). Performing an acute experiment (dashed grey line)\nonly provides a single snapshot of the developmental trajectory of an individual organism (or cell). Alternatively, a\nlongitudinal experiment (grey arrow) allows to track the properties of the same individual (or cell) throughout development,\nwhich is especially important in the case of variability in developmental trajectories (right).\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 4 of 46\nApplying the algorithm to a dataset recorded during the second postnatal week in mouse barrel\ncortex, a critical period for the formation of topographic maps in that region, yielded hundreds of\nidentified neurons tracked across all days. Assessing the quality of the algorithm by benchmarking\nit on a newly generated ground truth dataset showed high tracking performance 20     . Leveraging\nground truth benchmarking, we demonstrate that explicitly accounting for developmental\nprocesses, such as brain growth, are critical for accurately tracking cells during postnatal\ndevelopment. Our work thus shows that chronic calcium imaging and cell tracking using Track2p\ncan be used to monitor the changing physiological properties of large populations of matched\nneurons during early cortical development. We demonstrate that the statistics of activity patterns\nin the tracked population display two periods of stability, with a critical transition point around\npostnatal day 11 (P11), marking the emergence of a stable behavioral state representation.\nResults\nCell tracking using image registration and overlap-based matching\nTracking neuronal activity across multiple days presents unique challenges due to the dynamic\nnature of brain development. We developed a novel tracking algorithm, called Track2p. As in\nother tracking algorithms for calcium imaging data 13     ,16     ,21     , the final goal of Track2p is to\nfollow individual cells across sessions allowing the user to compare their functional properties in\ndownstream analyses. To achieve this goal, the algorithm takes as input a set of preprocessed\nrecordings, each consisting of a set of regions of interest (ROIs i.e. putative neurons detected based\non activity, see Methods) and their respective calcium fluorescence traces, as well as a mean image\nof the field of view (FOV). Briefly, the algorithm aims to match ROIs in any given pair of\nconsecutive sessions based on their spatial overlap, assuming that the more the two overlap in\nanatomical space, the more likely they correspond to the same neuron. Due to developmental\nprocesses such as brain growth and other experimental factors, it is necessary to account for day-\nto-day changes that occur between the two recordings before computing spatial overlaps. This is\nachieved by performing affine image registration on the mean FOV images between consecutive\ndays.\nWe apply the registration and spatial matching iteratively, starting with the first pair of sessions\n(s0 and s1) as follows (Fig. 2A     ):\nFirstly, we estimate the spatial transformation between s0 and s1 using affine image registration\n(Ntatsis 2023) (i.e. allowing shifting, rotation, scaling and shearing, see Fig. 2B     , the\ntransformation is denoted as T). We employ affine transformation, since it can account both for\nrigid transformations (rotations and translations arising from minor mismatches in FOV\nalignment across experiments) as well as scaling and shearing (mostly due to brain growth). The\nchanges across the two consecutive recordings are approximated as the transformation\nregistering the mean FOV from session s1 (green in Fig. 2B     ) to the mean FOV of s0 serving as\nreference (red in Fig. 2B     ).\nSecondly, the computed transformation is applied to the ROIs from session s1 (green in Fig. 2C     )\nto align them to ROIs from session s0 (red in Fig. 2C     ). The amount of spatial overlap after\nregistration (yellow in Fig. 2C     , bottom), can indicate the accuracy of the estimated\ntransformation between the two days. Assuming that the transformation is accurately estimated,\nROIs corresponding to the same cells display substantial spatial overlap, with some ROIs from one\nrecording also potentially overlapping poorly, if not at all, with any ROI from another.\nThirdly, once the ROIs are aligned, the algorithm proceeds with the matching (Fig. 2D     ). This is\ndone by computing a spatial similarity metric (intersection over union - IoU) between each ROI\nfrom session s0 and each transformed ROI from session s1. Matches are then assigned in a globally\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 5 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 5 of 46\nFigure 2\nThe Track2p pipeline for tracking cells across recordings.\nA: Schematic overview of the Track2p algorithm and the GUI capabilities. Dashed squares represent optional steps in the\npipeline. B: The transformation (T) between two consecutive days is computed by registering the mean FOV images. Top: An\noverlay of the reference (red, s0) and the moving (green, s1) images before registration. Bottom: Same two images after\nregistration. Scale bar: 50 μm. C: Applying the transform to ROIs from Suite2p segmentation. The ROI color code is the same\nas in B, the intersection of the ROIs is shown in yellow. Top: Overlap before T. Bottom: Overlap after T. Note: Only a few\nexample ROIs are displayed for explanation purposes. D: Cell matching using linear sum assignment. ROIs are the same as C\nwith indexes for the two recordings added for illustrative purposes. Cells from one recording are matched to cells from\nanother maximising the sum of the intersection over union (IoU) across all matches. E: Filtering putative false and true\nmatches by thresholding the IoU distribution. Top: The distribution of IoU values for matched ROI pairs shows a bimodal\ndistribution, which is used to reject putative false positive matches. Bottom: Final result of the cell linking for a pair of\nrecordings. F: Top: In the case of tracking across more than 2 imaging sessions, the steps from B to D are repeated\nsequentially to link the cells across all days. Bottom: Example matches for five cells (rows) successfully tracked across five\nconsecutive days (columns). Note: The figures shown are for illustrative purposes only, see Fig. 3      for application to real\ndata.\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 6 of 46\noptimal way by maximising the sum of IoU values across all matches using a linear sum\nassignment algorithm (Crouse 2016).\nFinally, since two consecutive sessions contain different sets of detected cells (see * in Fig. 2D     ,\nbottom) and since ROIs can overlap even if the signal does not come from the same cells (see + in\nFig. 2D     , bottom) we perform an additional filtering step on the assigned matches. Assuming that\nthe IoU values for putative true and putative false matches come from different distributions, we\nwould expect a bimodal distribution of IoU values across all assigned matches (see histogram in\nFig. 2E     ). To reject the putative false positives, we compute a threshold based on automatic\nthresholding methods (Otsu’s method 24     ). This ensures that assigned matches with low spatial\nsimilarity are rejected (see + in Fig. 2D and E     ) while the matches with high similarity are\naccepted (see x in Fig. 2D and E     ). This yields the final matching for the first pair of consecutive\nimaging sessions (s0 and s1). In the case of more than 2 recordings, this tracking procedure is then\niteratively applied for all consecutive pairs of sessions (s0 to s1, s1 to s2 … sN-1 to sN, and so on),\nwith tracks being extended sequentially from s0 to sN based on the identified matches, and\nterminated if a match was not identified at any particular session (Fig. 2F     ).\nWe provide an open-source implementation of the algorithm, combined with a user-friendly\ngraphical user interface (GUI), allowing non-specialist users to run the algorithm and interact with\nits outputs (see Fig. 2A      top, Fig. S1      and Supp. Info.: Software). Both the algorithm and the GUI\ncome with a simple installation procedure and extensive documentation to facilitate the ease of\nuse and accessibility of Track2p.\nTracking neurons across days in the early postnatal neocortex\nIn order to validate our method on experimental data, we next applied Track2p to a longitudinal\ndataset consisting of daily recordings of the same 720×720 μm FOV throughout the second\npostnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development (postnatal days 8 (P8) to P14, n=7 imaging\nsessions from one mouse; for more details see Methods.) In addition to the calcium indicator\n(GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic\nneurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice 22     . This dual-labelling strategy\nfacilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for\nTrack2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3     , for comparison with tracking\nbased on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before\nrunning Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p 23      for active cell detection\n(segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suite2p\n(ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\nThe Track2p algorithm appeared to successfully register the mean FOV for each successive pair of\nimaging sessions (based on visual inspection of Track2p outputs; for the first and last pairs see Fig.","numLines":200,"startLine":1,"totalLines":1601}}}

'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p'):\n- Contains the neural data for the successfully tracked neurons for the session day in Suite2p format\n- This neural data is obtained by running Track2p using the 'save the outputs in suite2p format'\n- For more information on the Suite2p format see: https://suite2p.readthedocs.io/en/latest/outputs.html\n- For more information on the Suite2p format output of Track2p see: https://track2p.github.io/outputs_suite2p.html \n- Briefly the data only includes traces for the cells present across all days, meaning that the activity matrix (for example F.npy or spks.npy, are of shape n_neurons x n_tstamps) will have the same number of rows (neurons) each day and the rows will be matched (e. g. first row of matrix 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy' corresponds to the same neuron as the first row of the matrix on the next recording day 'jm031/2023-10-19_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy')\n- This data can also be visualised using the Suite2p GUI, for example by opening two recordings of the same mouse in two windows - in that case (thanks to the reindexing) the color code will be the same across the two days for the same neuron.\n\nmove_deve sub-sub-folder (e. g. To assess the tracking\n   241→performance in a more quantitative and objective way, we next benchmarked Track2p’s\n   242→performance on manually generated ground truth for our specific experimental setting.\n   243→Benchmarking on manually tracked cells\n   244→In order to benchmark Track2p, expert annotators manually tracked cells across sessions\n   245→(similarly as in the Cell Tracking Challenge 20     ,25     ). Benchmarking was performed based on a\n   246→dataset from 3 mouse pups imaged under the same experimental conditions as described in the\n   247→previous section (including the dataset shown in Fig. 3     ). For each experiment, we chose 64\n   248→homogeneously distributed ROIs detected on the first day and tracked them based on visual\n   249→inspection across consecutive sessions (for more details see Methods and Fig. S3     ). This left us\n   250→with on average 20 neurons per experiment that we were able to manually track across all days\n   251→(‘GT’ in Fig 4     .). We then proceeded to compare these to cell tracks identified by Track2p. For the\n   252→purposes of evaluation we used a fully automatic tracking procedure (without manual curation\n   253→and with default Track2p parameters).\n   254→Different metrics exist for evaluating cell tracking. Since we are aiming to track cells across all\n   255→days, a robust cell tracking metric should reward perfect track matches with ground truth while\n   256→imposing penalties for missed or incorrectly identified tracks. For this reason we used a\n   257→biologically inspired ‘complete tracks’ quality metric, which corresponds to the F1 score for\n   258→completely reconstructed full tracks (we refer to this value as ‘CT’ according to 20     ,25     ). A CT\n   259→score of 1 would correspond to perfect tracking, while a CT score of 0 would correspond to no\n   260→matches or a large proportion of wrong matches (see Fig. 4A      and Methods for more details).\n   261→Additionally, to assess tracking performance over time, we quantified the proportion of\n   262→reconstructed ground truth tracks over progressively longer time intervals (first two days, first\n   263→three days etc. ‘Prop. correct’ in Fig. 4C-F     , see Methods). This allowed us to understand how\n   264→tracking accuracy depends on the number of successive sessions, as well as at which time points\n   265→the algorithm might fail to successfully track cells.\n   266→Track2p was evaluated under three different scenarios, and compared to the performance of a\n   267→widely used algorithm developed for longitudinal tracking in the adult brain (CellReg 21     , see\n   268→Methods). The initial evaluation was done using day-to-day registration based on a sparse\n   269→anatomical marker (as in Fig. 3     ). Calculating the CT score in this condition showed remarkably\n   270→high tracking performance for all datasets (see ‘Anatomical’ in Fig. 4B     , mean CT = 0.93). To test\n   271→whether a sparse marker was strictly necessary for successful tracking, we next ran the algorithm\n   272→using the mean image of the GCaMP8m channel as a comparison. Interestingly, this evaluation\n   273→showed a similar performance (see ‘Functional’ in Fig. 4B     , mean CT = 0.91), indicating that\n   274→dense calcium indicator labelling alone can provide comparable tracking performance,\n   275→eliminating the requirement for sparse anatomical labeling.\n   276→To show the importance of a method tailored to the growing brain, we next compared the baseline\n   277→tracking performance with two alternative conditions: firstly, by using Track2p without explicitly\n   278→accounting for day-to-day growth, performing a rigid instead of affine registration of consecutive\n   279→recordings; and secondly, by comparing Track2p to CellReg tracking 21     . In both scenarios,\n   280→Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 9 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 9 of 46\n   281→Figure 4\n   282→Evaluation of tracking performance on a manually tracked ground truth dataset\n   283→during the second postnatal week of development in the mouse neocortex.\n   284→A: A schematic representation of possible cases when comparing ground truth tracks (GT, solid lines) with those\n   285→reconstructed by Track2p (T2P, dashed lines). Interestingly however, these\n   294→methods still managed to correctly reconstruct a portion of tracks across shorter age spans in\n   295→certain cases (Fig. 4E and F     ), with the performance dropping significantly for longer tracks in\n   296→comparison to the baseline condition (Fig. 4C and D     ).\n   297→These comparisons demonstrate Track2p’s robust cell tracking performance in the developing\n   298→brain, maintaining high accuracy over extended periods of development. However, tracking\n   299→performance certainly depends on the type of experimental data (age, model system, brain area,\n   300→imaging parameters, FOV alignment etc.), hence we also provide additional resources and\n   301→documentation (https://track2p.github.io/home.html     ), allowing users to benchmark Track2p\n   302→tracking for their particular use case.\n   303→Development of firing statistics from hundreds of\n   304→tracked neurons across postnatal development\n   305→Having validated the performance of cell tracking, we next analyzed the development of\n   306→functional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of\n   307→mouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a\n   308→minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B      for\n   309→summary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons\n   310→per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 %\n   311→std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. Based on the high tracking\n   312→performance on ground truth (see Fig 4     ) we conclude that this drop is due to a failure in\n   313→detection rather than tracking. During the course of the experiment, the weight of a mouse\n   314→increased on average by 55% (± 18% std), with the pairwise distance between neurons increasing\n   315→on average by 15% (± 7% std) (Fig. 5C      and Fig. S5F     ). Weight was found to vary significantly\n   316→across mice, as early as the first experimental day, and to correlate with brain growth (r=0.92, Fig.\n   317→S5E     ). Our robust tracking capability provides a comprehensive view of the evolution of\n   318→neuronal dynamics within single mice, while also allowing us to take into account the\n   319→heterogeneity of the developmental timelines across mice.\n   320→The early postnatal period of cortical development studied here is characterized by the transition\n   321→from synchronous calcium events recruiting many neurons to progressively more decorrelated\n   322→population dynamics 26     ,27     . In order to visualise these global changes in our dataset of\n   323→longitudinally imaged mice, we first plotted rasterplots of calcium fluorescence traces as a\n   324→function of time for all tracked neurons on each imaging day in our example mouse (Fig. 5A      for\n   325→P8 and P14, all days in Fig. S4A     ). Visual inspection of these rasterplots clearly indicates the\n   326→disappearance of recurring periods of highly synchronous activity from around P11 onwards as\n   327→well as a global increase in single-neuron activity rates. Accordingly, quantifying calcium event\n   328→rates as a function of age indicated a gradual increase in the mean and a widening of the\n   329→distribution with age (Fig. 5D      and Fig. S5A      and G     , all statistical comparisons were done\n   330→between an ‘early’ (≤ P11) and ‘late’ (> P11) set of recordings). This evolution also signalled the\n   331→transition towards long-tailed firing rate distributions, which are ubiquitous in adult brain circuits\n   332→28     .\n   333→Next, we analyzed the distributions of pairwise correlations between activity traces from all\n   334→longitudinally tracked neurons as a function of mouse age (Fig. 5D      and Fig. S5B      and H     ).\n   335→Consistent with the disappearance of highly synchronous network events observed in the\n   336→rasterplots, we found a significant decrease in mean pairwise correlations, indicating a\n   337→progressive decorrelation of neuronal activity (Fig. 5E     ). The spatial distribution of pairwise\n   338→correlations also evolved across days from highly correlated locally to more broadly distributed\n   339→(Fig. 5F     ), suggesting the gradual breakdown of spatially clustered cell assemblies 5     .\n   340→Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 11 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 11 of 46\n   341→Figure 5\n   342→Evolution of neuronal activity statistics from hundreds of tracked\n   343→neurons during the second postnatal week of mouse development.\n   344→A: Raster plots showing the activity of all 728 tracked neurons as a function of time for the example mouse at P8 (top) and\n   345→P14 (bottom). Interestingly, we observed an increase in the number of\n   365→components required to explain a fixed amount of variance in the neural data across days,\n   366→suggesting a developmental increase in dimensionality (Fig. 5G     ), consistent with the statistics of\n   367→spontaneous activity described in the adult brain 29     . Notably, the plots of summary statistics for\n   368→our entire dataset, indicated a clear outlier, consistent across all quantifications (Fig. 5D, E, F and\n   369→G     ). This outlier mouse displayed a similar but delayed developmental trajectory, compared to\n   370→the other mice. Interestingly, it also showed the lowest initial weight and a less pronounced\n   371→growth in weight and cortical size (Fig. 5C     ), likely indicating a lower maturation stage at the\n   372→onset of the experiments, although the contribution of other experimental factors cannot be\n   373→excluded given the invasiveness of imaging surgery.\n   374→To fully leverage our longitudinal approach, we next turned to comparing the functional\n   375→properties of the same neurons across development, which is only possible when tracking cells\n   376→across days.\n   377→A marked reorganisation of the functional network\n   378→structure during the second postnatal week\n   379→We first examined the stability of the correlation structure, which we will refer to as ‘Functional\n   380→connectivity’ (FC). Two alternative possibilities could be envisaged (see Fig. 6A     ,: (i) FC could be\n   381→conserved across days, meaning that a highly connected pair on a given day would remain highly\n   382→connected on the next (Fig. 6A     , left); (ii) FC could reorganise, losing the structure from the\n   383→previous day (Fig. 6A     , right). In the first case, the FC values for all pairs would remain similar\n   384→across the two days (Fig. 6A     , bottom left) whereas in the second case they would be different\n   385→(Fig. 6A     , bottom right). To discriminate between the two cases, we defined a ‘FC similarity’ score\n   386→for any pair of imaging days with matched neurons for a given mouse (Pearson correlation (r)\n   387→across all neuron pairs, see Fig. 6A     , bottom). Such FC similarity metric, encapsulates a variety of\n   388→possible sources of network changes, from local or long-range connectivity, intrinsic excitability to\n   389→neuromodulation.\n   390→Quantifying FC similarity across two early sessions for an example animal (P8 to P9, Fig. 6B      top\n   391→left) indicated remarkable stability, almost identical to the FC similarity within a given imaging\n   392→day session which we took as a reference (computed as FC similarity between the first and second\n   393→half of a same day recording, Fig 6B     , bottom left). Conversely when comparing across more\n   394→distant developmental ages, we noticed that a large part of the correlation structure was lost,\n   395→resulting in lower FC similarity (P8 to P14, Fig. 6B     , top middle). Interestingly, FC similarity\n   396→between a pair of later developmental sessions, was again comparable to within-session similarity\n   397→(P13 to P14 and P14 within, Fig. 6B     , right), indicating that the functional network structure was\n   398→stable, but different from that of earlier ages. Of note, within session stability was consistently\n   399→lower later than earlier. Quantifying calcium event rate similarity across days indicated a similar\n   400→pattern (see Fig. S6F-J     ).\n   401→To investigate this further we proceeded to compute the FC similarity for all possible combinations\n   402→of sessions in all mice. Interestingly we observed a sigmoid-like decay of FC similarity when taking\n   403→P8 as a reference (Fig. 6C     ). Plotting the full FC similarity matrix for all possible combinations of\n   404→sessions also indicated two stable FC regimes with seemingly two blocks along the diagonal (Fig.\n   405→6D      left for example, 6D      right for average and 6E for statistical comparison). Interestingly, the\n   406→sharpness of the transition in FC varied across mice (Fig. S6C     ) and could in part be explained by\n   407→the weight of the mouse at the onset of the experiment (Fig. 6C     , linear part of sigmoid fit). Such\n   408→variation across mice could reflect either inherent individual differences in developmental\n   409→processes or experimental factors, where mice with a lower initial weight may have experienced\n   410→delayed development due to higher sensitivity to the imaging procedure.\n   411→Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 13 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 13 of 46\n   412→Figure 6\n   413→Transition between two stable functional network structures\n   414→during the second postnatal week of barrel cortex development.\n   415→A: Schematic explanation of the framework to compare functional connectivity across days; grey nodes represent neurons,\n   416→strength of a functional connection is denoted by the opacity of the blue edge between two nodes. For a network of 4\n   417→neurons and a given connectivity Cd (16 connections, top) we can imagine that the connectivity on the next day (Cd+1) could\n   418→be ‘conserved’ (middle left) or it could be ‘reorganised’ (middle right). A scatter plot comparing all 16 functional connections\n   419→between Cd and Cd+1 would indicate a high correlation in the ‘conserved’ case (bottom left) and low correlation in the\n   420→reorganised case (bottom right). We refer to this correlation as ‘FC similarity’. B: Scatter plots of FC for three different pairs of\n   421→recording days: a pair of early sessions (P8 to P9, top left), an early and a late session (P8 to P14, top middle) and a pair of late\n   422→sessions (P13 to P14, top right). Within-session FC similarity (bottom scatter plots) i.e. comparing the first and second half of\n   423→an imaging session at early (P81/2 to P82/2, bottom left) and late ages (P141/2 to P142/2, bottom right). For visualisation\n   424→purposes a random subset of 200 pairs is displayed; Linear fit and r (pearson correlation or ‘FC similarity’) are computed on\n   425→all pairs; Symbols next to r values indicate the same values in panels C and D. E: Box plots comparing within day (left) or across-day (right) FC similarity\n   431→for early (≤ P11) and late (> P11) developmental epochs pooled across all mice. *: statistically significant; ns: not statistically\n   432→significant (Kruskall-Wallis test: p = 3.6 × 10−22; post-hoc Mann–Whitney U tests with Bonferroni correction, early within - late\n   433→within: p = 3.3 × 10−3, early to early - late to late: p = n. s., early to early - early to late: p = 1.7 × 10−12, late to late - early to late:\n   434→p = 4.2 × 10−5, for all possible comparisons see Fig. S6K     ).\n   435→Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 14 of 46\n   436→Altogether, our analyses indicate that the second postnatal week marks a transition between two\n   437→stable functional network structures in the barrel cortex of developing mice. Additionally, this\n   438→shows how tracking cells during development opens new analysis possibilities using self-\n   439→referencing between neurons, providing new insights into how the developmental choreography\n   440→unfolds in the same population of neurons across days.\n   441→Developmental emergence of stable behavioral state modulation\n   442→As a first attempt to investigate the mechanisms underlying this network transition, we examined\n   443→neural activity in relation to behavioral state. The second postnatal week marks the emergence of\n   444→active sensation, suggesting that changes in arousal state modulation might drive this network\n   445→reorganization. In the adult cortex, arousal strongly shapes both global firing rates and neural\n   446→correlations 30     –33     , making it a promising candidate for orchestrating the developmental shift\n   447→we observed.\n   448→To this aim, we sorted the neurons based on the similarity of their activity patterns, using\n   449→Rastermap 34     , and examined the relationship between neuronal activity and behavioral states.\n   450→We assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse\n   451→movement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric (as in 30     , for more details see\n   452→Methods). Interestingly, this sorting revealed a subpopulation of neurons that were highly\n   453→correlated with the animal’s motion at later, but not earlier developmental stages (Fig. 5A     , see\n   454→Fig. S4A      for all days). We first quantified this relationship by computing the temporal\n   455→correlation between the first principal component of neuronal activity with the animal’s motion.\n   456→Interestingly, this correlation showed a steep increase after P11 for most mice (Fig. 7D     ). This\n   457→suggested that global fluctuations in neural activity are more strongly modulated by active\n   458→movement at later than earlier developmental stages.\n   459→Finally, we asked whether behavioral state could be decoded from population activity dynamics\n   460→using regression analysis. Despite substantial variability, the same-day decoding performance\n   461→increased with development (Fig. 7A-C     ). Since we tracked the same neurons across days, we\n   462→could also probe the stability of this representation across days, by fitting a model on one day and\n   463→testing it on all other days (cross-day decoding, Fig. 7E-H     , Fig. S7B      and C     15     ,35     ).\n   464→Interestingly, this analysis showed that, once developed, this representation was indeed stable,\n   465→allowing for accurate cross-day decoding, with the same neurons showing either consistently\n   466→positive or negative modulation across days (see Fig. S7D      for the activity traces of an example\n   467→cell with a high weight for decoding).\n   468→Hence, by combining Track2p longitudinal cell tracking with decoding approaches, one can\n   469→systematically map the emergence and stability of cortical representations in the developing\n   470→brain.\n   471→Discussion\n   472→Here we described Track2P, a cell tracking algorithm that can be used to follow the changing\n   473→dynamics of hundreds of matched neurons from daily calcium imaging recordings in the growing\n   474→brain of living mouse pups. At the core of the method lies sequential affine registration followed\n   475→by cell matching for each subsequent pair of recordings, which lead to a better tracking\n   476→performance during development when using Track2p compared to other methods. Using this\n   477→benchmarked approach, we observed a sharp developmental transition from highly synchronized\n   478→activity to multidimensional, behavior-state dependent neural dynamics. Beyond this key finding,\n   479→our method opens a much needed tool for investigating the developmental functional trajectories\n   480→of individual neurons during early postnatal brain development, and their deviations caused by\n   481→genetic mutations or environmental perturbations.\n   482→Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 15 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 15 of 46\n   483→Figure 7\n   484→Regression analysis to study the development and stability of neural representations.\n   485→A: Left: A decoding model is fitted on each day to predict a behavioural variable (y, mouse motion) from the simultaneously\n   486→recorded calcium imaging data (X, activity raster). Registering\nthe ROIs for each pair of consecutive recordings showed a great degree of overlap for the majority\nof ROIs (yellow area in Fig. 3A and B      bottom). We did however observe several ROIs that were\nonly present in one session from the pair, likely due to differences in cell detection and\ndevelopmental factors such as growth, silencing or cell death. When matching ROIs across sessions\nusing spatial overlap (IoU), we would expect these ROIs to show significantly lower IoU values\ncompared to the cells that were present in both sessions. To assess the tracking\nperformance in a more quantitative and objective way, we next benchmarked Track2p’s\nperformance on manually generated ground truth for our specific experimental setting.\nBenchmarking on manually tracked cells\nIn order to benchmark Track2p, expert annotators manually tracked cells across sessions\n(similarly as in the Cell Tracking Challenge 20     ,25     ). Benchmarking was performed based on a\ndataset from 3 mouse pups imaged under the same experimental conditions as described in the\nprevious section (including the dataset shown in Fig. 3     ). For each experiment, we chose 64\nhomogeneously distributed ROIs detected on the first day and tracked them based on visual\ninspection across consecutive sessions (for more details see Methods and Fig. S3     ). This left us\nwith on average 20 neurons per experiment that we were able to manually track across all days\n(‘GT’ in Fig 4     .). We then proceeded to compare these to cell tracks identified by Track2p. For the\npurposes of evaluation we used a fully automatic tracking procedure (without manual curation\nand with default Track2p parameters).\nDifferent metrics exist for evaluating cell tracking. Since we are aiming to track cells across all\ndays, a robust cell tracking metric should reward perfect track matches with ground truth while\nimposing penalties for missed or incorrectly identified tracks. For this reason we used a\nbiologically inspired ‘complete tracks’ quality metric, which corresponds to the F1 score for\ncompletely reconstructed full tracks (we refer to this value as ‘CT’ according to 20     ,25     ). A CT\nscore of 1 would correspond to perfect tracking, while a CT score of 0 would correspond to no\nmatches or a large proportion of wrong matches (see Fig. 4A      and Methods for more details).\nAdditionally, to assess tracking performance over time, we quantified the proportion of\nreconstructed ground truth tracks over progressively longer time intervals (first two days, first\nthree days etc. ‘Prop. correct’ in Fig. 4C-F     , see Methods). This allowed us to understand how\ntracking accuracy depends on the number of successive sessions, as well as at which time points\nthe algorithm might fail to successfully track cells.\nTrack2p was evaluated under three different scenarios, and compared to the performance of a\nwidely used algorithm developed for longitudinal tracking in the adult brain (CellReg 21     , see\nMethods). The initial evaluation was done using day-to-day registration based on a sparse\nanatomical marker (as in Fig. 3     ). Calculating the CT score in this condition showed remarkably\nhigh tracking performance for all datasets (see ‘Anatomical’ in Fig. 4B     , mean CT = 0.93). To test\nwhether a sparse marker was strictly necessary for successful tracking, we next ran the algorithm\nusing the mean image of the GCaMP8m channel as a comparison. Interestingly, this evaluation\nshowed a similar performance (see ‘Functional’ in Fig. 4B     , mean CT = 0.91), indicating that\ndense calcium indicator labelling alone can provide comparable tracking performance,\neliminating the requirement for sparse anatomical labeling.\nTo show the importance of a method tailored to the growing brain, we next compared the baseline\ntracking performance with two alternative conditions: firstly, by using Track2p without explicitly\naccounting for day-to-day growth, performing a rigid instead of affine registration of consecutive\nrecordings; and secondly, by comparing Track2p to CellReg tracking 21     . In both scenarios,\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 9 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 9 of 46\nFigure 4\nEvaluation of tracking performance on a manually tracked ground truth dataset\nduring the second postnatal week of development in the mouse neocortex.\nA: A schematic representation of possible cases when comparing ground truth tracks (GT, solid lines) with those\nreconstructed by Track2p (T2P, dashed lines). Interestingly however, these\nmethods still managed to correctly reconstruct a portion of tracks across shorter age spans in\ncertain cases (Fig. 4E and F     ), with the performance dropping significantly for longer tracks in\ncomparison to the baseline condition (Fig. 4C and D     ).\nThese comparisons demonstrate Track2p’s robust cell tracking performance in the developing\nbrain, maintaining high accuracy over extended periods of development. However, tracking\nperformance certainly depends on the type of experimental data (age, model system, brain area,\nimaging parameters, FOV alignment etc.), hence we also provide additional resources and\ndocumentation (https://track2p.github.io/home.html     ), allowing users to benchmark Track2p\ntracking for their particular use case.\nDevelopment of firing statistics from hundreds of\ntracked neurons across postnatal development\nHaving validated the performance of cell tracking, we next analyzed the development of\nfunctional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of\nmouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a\nminimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B      for\nsummary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons\nper mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 %\nstd) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. Based on the high tracking\nperformance on ground truth (see Fig 4     ) we conclude that this drop is due to a failure in\ndetection rather than tracking. During the course of the experiment, the weight of a mouse\nincreased on average by 55% (± 18% std), with the pairwise distance between neurons increasing\non average by 15% (± 7% std) (Fig. 5C      and Fig. S5F     ). Weight was found to vary significantly\nacross mice, as early as the first experimental day, and to correlate with brain growth (r=0.92, Fig.\nS5E     ). Our robust tracking capability provides a comprehensive view of the evolution of\nneuronal dynamics within single mice, while also allowing us to take into account the\nheterogeneity of the developmental timelines across mice.\nThe early postnatal period of cortical development studied here is characterized by the transition\nfrom synchronous calcium events recruiting many neurons to progressively more decorrelated\npopulation dynamics 26     ,27     . In order to visualise these global changes in our dataset of\nlongitudinally imaged mice, we first plotted rasterplots of calcium fluorescence traces as a\nfunction of time for all tracked neurons on each imaging day in our example mouse (Fig. 5A      for\nP8 and P14, all days in Fig. S4A     ). Visual inspection of these rasterplots clearly indicates the\ndisappearance of recurring periods of highly synchronous activity from around P11 onwards as\nwell as a global increase in single-neuron activity rates. Accordingly, quantifying calcium event\nrates as a function of age indicated a gradual increase in the mean and a widening of the\ndistribution with age (Fig. 5D      and Fig. S5A      and G     , all statistical comparisons were done\nbetween an ‘early’ (≤ P11) and ‘late’ (> P11) set of recordings). This evolution also signalled the\ntransition towards long-tailed firing rate distributions, which are ubiquitous in adult brain circuits\n28     .\nNext, we analyzed the distributions of pairwise correlations between activity traces from all\nlongitudinally tracked neurons as a function of mouse age (Fig. 5D      and Fig. S5B      and H     ).\nConsistent with the disappearance of highly synchronous network events observed in the\nrasterplots, we found a significant decrease in mean pairwise correlations, indicating a\nprogressive decorrelation of neuronal activity (Fig. 5E     ). The spatial distribution of pairwise\ncorrelations also evolved across days from highly correlated locally to more broadly distributed\n(Fig. 5F     ), suggesting the gradual breakdown of spatially clustered cell assemblies 5     .\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 11 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 11 of 46\nFigure 5\nEvolution of neuronal activity statistics from hundreds of tracked\nneurons during the second postnatal week of mouse development.\nA: Raster plots showing the activity of all 728 tracked neurons as a function of time for the example mouse at P8 (top) and\nP14 (bottom). Interestingly, we observed an increase in the number of\ncomponents required to explain a fixed amount of variance in the neural data across days,\nsuggesting a developmental increase in dimensionality (Fig. 5G     ), consistent with the statistics of\nspontaneous activity described in the adult brain 29     . Notably, the plots of summary statistics for\nour entire dataset, indicated a clear outlier, consistent across all quantifications (Fig. 5D, E, F and\nG     ). This outlier mouse displayed a similar but delayed developmental trajectory, compared to\nthe other mice. Interestingly, it also showed the lowest initial weight and a less pronounced\ngrowth in weight and cortical size (Fig. 5C     ), likely indicating a lower maturation stage at the\nonset of the experiments, although the contribution of other experimental factors cannot be\nexcluded given the invasiveness of imaging surgery.\nTo fully leverage our longitudinal approach, we next turned to comparing the functional\nproperties of the same neurons across development, which is only possible when tracking cells\nacross days.\nA marked reorganisation of the functional network\nstructure during the second postnatal week\nWe first examined the stability of the correlation structure, which we will refer to as ‘Functional\nconnectivity’ (FC). Two alternative possibilities could be envisaged (see Fig. 6A     ,: (i) FC could be\nconserved across days, meaning that a highly connected pair on a given day would remain highly\nconnected on the next (Fig. 6A     , left); (ii) FC could reorganise, losing the structure from the\nprevious day (Fig. 6A     , right). In the first case, the FC values for all pairs would remain similar\nacross the two days (Fig. 6A     , bottom left) whereas in the second case they would be different\n(Fig. 6A     , bottom right). To discriminate between the two cases, we defined a ‘FC similarity’ score\nfor any pair of imaging days with matched neurons for a given mouse (Pearson correlation (r)\nacross all neuron pairs, see Fig. 6A     , bottom). Such FC similarity metric, encapsulates a variety of\npossible sources of network changes, from local or long-range connectivity, intrinsic excitability to\nneuromodulation.\nQuantifying FC similarity across two early sessions for an example animal (P8 to P9, Fig. 6B      top\nleft) indicated remarkable stability, almost identical to the FC similarity within a given imaging\nday session which we took as a reference (computed as FC similarity between the first and second\nhalf of a same day recording, Fig 6B     , bottom left). Conversely when comparing across more\ndistant developmental ages, we noticed that a large part of the correlation structure was lost,\nresulting in lower FC similarity (P8 to P14, Fig. 6B     , top middle). Interestingly, FC similarity\nbetween a pair of later developmental sessions, was again comparable to within-session similarity\n(P13 to P14 and P14 within, Fig. 6B     , right), indicating that the functional network structure was\nstable, but different from that of earlier ages. Of note, within session stability was consistently\nlower later than earlier. Quantifying calcium event rate similarity across days indicated a similar\npattern (see Fig. S6F-J     ).\nTo investigate this further we proceeded to compute the FC similarity for all possible combinations\nof sessions in all mice. Interestingly we observed a sigmoid-like decay of FC similarity when taking\nP8 as a reference (Fig. 6C     ). Plotting the full FC similarity matrix for all possible combinations of\nsessions also indicated two stable FC regimes with seemingly two blocks along the diagonal (Fig.\n6D      left for example, 6D      right for average and 6E for statistical comparison). Interestingly, the\nsharpness of the transition in FC varied across mice (Fig. S6C     ) and could in part be explained by\nthe weight of the mouse at the onset of the experiment (Fig. 6C     , linear part of sigmoid fit). Such\nvariation across mice could reflect either inherent individual differences in developmental\nprocesses or experimental factors, where mice with a lower initial weight may have experienced\ndelayed development due to higher sensitivity to the imaging procedure.\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 13 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 13 of 46\nFigure 6\nTransition between two stable functional network structures\nduring the second postnatal week of barrel cortex development.\nA: Schematic explanation of the framework to compare functional connectivity across days; grey nodes represent neurons,\nstrength of a functional connection is denoted by the opacity of the blue edge between two nodes. For a network of 4\nneurons and a given connectivity Cd (16 connections, top) we can imagine that the connectivity on the next day (Cd+1) could\nbe ‘conserved’ (middle left) or it could be ‘reorganised’ (middle right). A scatter plot comparing all 16 functional connections\nbetween Cd and Cd+1 would indicate a high correlation in the ‘conserved’ case (bottom left) and low correlation in the\nreorganised case (bottom right). E: Box plots comparing within day (left) or across-day (right) FC similarity\nfor early (≤ P11) and late (> P11) developmental epochs pooled across all mice. *: statistically significant; ns: not statistically\nsignificant (Kruskall-Wallis test: p = 3.6 × 10−22; post-hoc Mann–Whitney U tests with Bonferroni correction, early within - late\nwithin: p = 3.3 × 10−3, early to early - late to late: p = n. s., early to early - early to late: p = 1.7 × 10−12, late to late - early to late:\np = 4.2 × 10−5, for all possible comparisons see Fig. S6K     ).\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 14 of 46\nAltogether, our analyses indicate that the second postnatal week marks a transition between two\nstable functional network structures in the barrel cortex of developing mice. Additionally, this\nshows how tracking cells during development opens new analysis possibilities using self-\nreferencing between neurons, providing new insights into how the developmental choreography\nunfolds in the same population of neurons across days.\nDevelopmental emergence of stable behavioral state modulation\nAs a first attempt to investigate the mechanisms underlying this network transition, we examined\nneural activity in relation to behavioral state. The second postnatal week marks the emergence of\nactive sensation, suggesting that changes in arousal state modulation might drive this network\nreorganization. In the adult cortex, arousal strongly shapes both global firing rates and neural\ncorrelations 30     –33     , making it a promising candidate for orchestrating the developmental shift\nwe observed.\nTo this aim, we sorted the neurons based on the similarity of their activity patterns, using\nRastermap 34     , and examined the relationship between neuronal activity and behavioral states.\nWe assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse\nmovement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric (as in 30     , for more details see\nMethods). Interestingly, this sorting revealed a subpopulation of neurons that were highly\ncorrelated with the animal’s motion at later, but not earlier developmental stages (Fig. 5A     , see\nFig. S4A      for all days). We first quantified this relationship by computing the temporal\ncorrelation between the first principal component of neuronal activity with the animal’s motion.\nInterestingly, this correlation showed a steep increase after P11 for most mice (Fig. 7D     ). This\nsuggested that global fluctuations in neural activity are more strongly modulated by active\nmovement at later than earlier developmental stages.\nFinally, we asked whether behavioral state could be decoded from population activity dynamics\nusing regression analysis. Despite substantial variability, the same-day decoding performance\nincreased with development (Fig. 7A-C     ). Since we tracked the same neurons across days, we\ncould also probe the stability of this representation across days, by fitting a model on one day and\ntesting it on all other days (cross-day decoding, Fig. 7E-H     , Fig. S7B      and C     15     ,35     ).\nInterestingly, this analysis showed that, once developed, this representation was indeed stable,\nallowing for accurate cross-day decoding, with the same neurons showing either consistently\npositive or negative modulation across days (see Fig. S7D      for the activity traces of an example\ncell with a high weight for decoding).\nHence, by combining Track2p longitudinal cell tracking with decoding approaches, one can\nsystematically map the emergence and stability of cortical representations in the developing\nbrain.\nDiscussion\nHere we described Track2P, a cell tracking algorithm that can be used to follow the changing\ndynamics of hundreds of matched neurons from daily calcium imaging recordings in the growing\nbrain of living mouse pups. At the core of the method lies sequential affine registration followed\nby cell matching for each subsequent pair of recordings, which lead to a better tracking\nperformance during development when using Track2p compared to other methods. Using this\nbenchmarked approach, we observed a sharp developmental transition from highly synchronized\nactivity to multidimensional, behavior-state dependent neural dynamics. Beyond this key finding,\nour method opens a much needed tool for investigating the developmental functional trajectories\nof individual neurons during early postnatal brain development, and their deviations caused by\ngenetic mutations or environmental perturbations.\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 15 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 15 of 46\nFigure 7\nRegression analysis to study the development and stability of neural representations.\nA: Left: A decoding model is fitted on each day to predict a behavioural variable (y, mouse motion) from the simultaneously\nrecorded calcium imaging data (X, activity raster). Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step \\u2014 (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction \\u2014 identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'output': [  # When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \\n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \\n3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\\n4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\\n  a.
